#2759. Start Your Online Pusoy Journey with These Apps!

Online Pusoy allows players to enjoy a long-standing Filipino card game without needing to gather a complete group in one location.

While the format has moved to digital platforms, the game’s foundation remains unchanged. Four players sit at a virtual table, receive 13 cards each, and arrange them into three separate hands before scoring begins.

The arrangement process is where the challenge starts. Every player must create a three-card front hand, a five-card middle hand, and a five-card back hand.

The back hand must be the strongest of the three, while the front hand must be the weakest.

Once all players finalize their cards, the platform compares each hand against the corresponding hands of the other participants and awards points based on the results.

Many newcomers first search for how to play Pusoy because the game looks deceptively simple. In reality, winning requires more than identifying poker combinations.

Players must determine where their strongest cards provide the greatest advantage, when to sacrifice one hand to strengthen another, and how to avoid exposing weaknesses across all three sections.

Digital platforms simplify the experience through automatic scoring, instant matchmaking, and player statistics.

Instead of manually calculating results, players can concentrate on decision-making and strategy. Many apps also include practice modes and historical records that help users review past performances.

Because the core rules remain intact, online versions continue to reward planning, observation, and risk management.

Whether someone is completely new to Pusoy or already familiar with its mechanics, digital play provides a practical way to gain experience while preserving the strategic depth that has kept the game relevant for generations.

For many players, Pusoy Go serves as an introduction to digital Pusoy.

The platform emphasizes accessibility, making it easy for users to enter matches and become familiar with the game’s mechanics.

Rather than overwhelming new players with complex systems, it presents a straightforward experience that encourages learning through regular play.

One of the platform’s strongest features is its social element. Players can communicate with others during matches, participate in club-based activities, and connect with communities centered around Pusoy.

These features help replicate the atmosphere of traditional card gatherings, where conversation and friendly competition often go hand in hand.

Pusoy Go also incorporates progression systems that provide additional motivation. Daily rewards, event participation, and achievement milestones encourage players to continue developing their skills.

At the same time, performance tracking tools allow users to measure improvement and identify areas where they can become more effective.

The platform’s flexibility appeals to different types of players. Beginners can use casual matches to understand card arrangements and scoring systems, while experienced users can experiment with new approaches and compare results.

Because matches are generally easy to join and complete, players can fit games into a variety of schedules.

Another advantage is the platform’s approachable design. Navigation remains simple, allowing players to focus on gameplay instead of spending time learning menus and systems.

This accessibility contributes to its popularity among players seeking an environment that balances learning, competition, and social interaction.

For those looking for a relaxed entry point into online Pusoy, Pusoy Go provides a setting where players can build confidence while enjoying the social side of the game.

Not every Pusoy player is interested in clubs, chat functions, or social rewards. Some prefer an environment that places strategy at the center of the experience.

Pusoy ZingPlay caters to that audience by focusing heavily on gameplay fundamentals and skill development.

Its design philosophy differs from many social-oriented platforms. The interface is intentionally streamlined, reducing distractions and allowing players to concentrate on hand construction and scoring decisions.

Every match encourages users to think carefully about card placement and long-term outcomes.

A major feature of ZingPlay is its AI practice system. Players can challenge computer opponents with varying difficulty levels, creating opportunities to test strategies and improve decision-making.

For beginners, these matches provide a controlled environment for learning the rules. For experienced players, they serve as a way to refine techniques without external pressure.

The platform also includes rankings, timed competitions, and statistical tracking. These tools help players evaluate progress and identify recurring mistakes.

Since success in Pusoy depends heavily on arranging cards efficiently across three hands, repeated practice can reveal valuable patterns that improve future performance.

Risk assessment becomes especially important during competitive matches. Players must balance aggressive arrangements against defensive setups while anticipating potential outcomes.

ZingPlay’s focus on consistency encourages users to develop these skills through repetition and analysis.

By prioritizing gameplay over social engagement, the platform creates a space where strategy remains the primary attraction.

Players who enjoy studying the finer details of Pusoy often appreciate the structured environment that ZingPlay provides.

Players who become comfortable with digital Pusoy may eventually seek a platform that offers more structured competition.

GameZone addresses that need by providing several Pusoy variants while maintaining the traditional gameplay that players already understand.

Its selection includes Pusoy Plus, Pusoy Wild, and Pusoy Jackpot. Pusoy Plus follows the standard format closely, making it a familiar choice for players who want a classic experience.

Pusoy Wild introduces unique conditions that require greater adaptability and planning. Pusoy Jackpot retains the game’s traditional structure while incorporating a performance-based reward component.

These options allow players to explore different styles of competition without abandoning the mechanics that define Pusoy.

Regardless of the variant selected, success still depends on strong card arrangement, sound judgment, and effective risk management.

GameZone also supports player development through match records and statistical tools. Users can review previous games, analyze results, and identify trends that may help improve future performance.

This information can be valuable for players who approach the game analytically and want measurable feedback.

The platform’s operational standards further distinguish it from many casual gaming environments.

GameZone operates under PAGCOR licensing and utilizes KYC verification alongside responsible gaming measures. These systems help support player security and fair competition.

For players looking to move beyond casual matches, GameZone provides a structured environment where traditional Pusoy meets organized competitive play.

With multiple variants, performance-tracking tools, and regulated operations, it offers several paths for players who want to continue developing their skills.

Q: What is Pusoy?A: Pusoy is a card game where players arrange 13 cards into three hands and compare them against opponents for points.

Q: How to play Pusoy?A: Arrange your cards into a three-card front hand, a five-card middle hand, and a five-card back hand, with the back hand being the strongest.

Q: How is Pusoy different from Pusoy Dos?A: Pusoy focuses on hand arrangement and scoring, while Pusoy Dos is a shedding game where players race to discard all cards.

Q: What Pusoy games are available on GameZone?A: GameZone offers Pusoy Plus, Pusoy Wild, and Pusoy Jackpot.

Q: Is GameZone legitimate?A: Yes. GameZone operates under PAGCOR licensing and implements KYC and responsible gaming measures.

Nel commentare l’azione, Marelli distingue il semplice tocco da un contatto vero e proprio. Richiama inoltre la trattenuta ripetuta e costante di Troilo ai danni di Frattesi.

Marelli ritiene comprensibile che l’arbitro possa aver giudicato regolare l’intervento in campo. Secondo la sua analisi, però, il VAR avrebbe dovuto intervenire.

#2754. Serie C, Inter U23-Scafatese 2-1 risultato finale: vittoria di carattere, agganciato il sesto posto

Serie C, Inter U23-Scafatese 2-1 risultato finale: vittoria di carattere, agganciato il sesto posto

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Vittoria di carattere per l'Inter U23, che batte 2-1 la Scafatese nel recupero dell'ottava giornata di campionato e sale a quota 17 punti, agganciando il sesto posto in classifica. Ottima prestazione da parte dei ragazzi di Vecchi, che interrompono la striscia positiva di otto risultati utili consecutivi degli avversari ed entrano pienamente in zona playoff. I nerazzurri partono forte, vanno vicini al gol e si vedono negare un calcio di rigore clamoroso, e nel primo quarto d'ora deve fare i conti con il problema muscolare di Iddrissou, appena rientrato da un infortunio. Nonostante tutto questo l'Inter passa comunque in vantaggio con il solito Zuberek, al sesto centro nelle ultime quattro partite. Neanche il tempo di festeggiare, però, che gli ospiti pareggiano con una grande giocata di Palmieri, che sorprende alle spalle Marello e Tosto.

Nella ripresa i nerazzurri continuano ad attaccare e trovano la rete del meritato 2-1 con una splendida punizione dal limite di Marello. Nel finale la Scafatese prova il tutto e per tutto, l'arbitro concede ben 7 minuti di recupero ma il risultato non cambia fino al triplice fischio.

Reti: 36' Zuberek (I), 39' Palmieri (S), 62' Marello (I)

#2753. Julian Alvarez apologizes to Atletico Madrid fans over his World Cup comments: ‘I would have been angry too’

Julian Alvarez has publicly addressed the controversy that followed his World Cup remarks, asking Atletico Madrid supporters for forgiveness. The Argentine forward spoke in an interview with Movistar Plus Deportes after giving his team a 1-0 win over Deportivo La Coruña at the Metropolitano.

“I wanted to apologize to our people for my words during the World Cup. They were a mistake on my part, both the way I said them and the timing, and above all I understand people’s anger,” Alvarez said. “As a fan, I would have been angry too, especially considering how they have always supported me over all these years and these last few weeks, when they have been very generous. I value that a lot”.

His comments during the tournament expressed a desire to leave the club, which did not sit well with the fans. The forward was heavily criticized by the supporters after his remarks, who made their frustration clear with boos at every home game and even demanded that he leave the team.

Even so, he kept working to find his best form again, and it was only against Stuttgart in the Champions League that he scored his first goal of the season. Against Deportivo La Coruna, he then became the hero by scoring his side’s only goal to secure three valuable points.

Alvarez also said he is feeling better on the pitch. “Little by little I’m feeling good. I thank all my teammates because they have supported me a lot during this time,” he said, adding that he feels sharper both with the ball and physically. “When you feel good in life, it shows on the pitch. It’s everything: family, I’ve spent a lot of time at home doing other things, and it all matters, but I feel good and ready to compete for everything.” Asked whether the whistles could turn into applause, he answered: “I hope so, with work and humility”.

Julian Alvarez joins Lionel Messi among Argentina’s all-time top Champions League scorers

The controversy began at the World Cup, when Alvarez spoke with Argentine media in the mixed zone after Argentina’s 2-0 win over Austria. The forward, who had reportedly been upset with how his club handled Barcelona’s $116 million bid, which went unanswered, confirmed that he wanted to leave.

“I don’t think it’s the right time to talk about this, but I can’t hide or pretend otherwise either. I try to be an honest person. I spoke with the people at the club, with those I needed to talk to,” Alvarez told a reporter. “I think a transfer is best for everyone, and I want to fulfill my dream”.

The comments left the Argentine in an uncomfortable spot, with the club’s supporters turning on him and the pressure building at every home game. His transfer never materialized, though, and he stayed in Madrid to face the consequences. Now, after finally finding the net again, he has chosen to own his words and ask the fans to turn the page.

Julian Alvarez ended a 174-day goal drought and joined Lionel Messi among Argentina’s top Champions League scorers.

The Argentina international had hoped to take the next step in his career, but the collapse of a high-profile transfer has now triggered a significant change behind the scenes, raising fresh questions about what comes next for the 26-year-old striker.

Real Madrid travel to the Metropolitano on Sunday looking to end a long winless run against city rivals Atletico Madrid, with Julian Alvarez's fitness the biggest storyline heading into the LaLiga clash.

Julian Alvarez will miss Atletico Madrid's LaLiga clash with Osasuna as he recovers from a muscle injury.

#2752. Frankfurt’s pressing adjustments secure victory over Galatasaray – MH

von Next Generation am 22.09.2025 in den Kategorien Champions League mit 1 Kommentar

Eintracht Frankfurt won their opening Champions League matchday with a 5-1 victory over Galatasaray Istanbul. After about ten minutes, Frankfurt were trailing 0-1, but thanks in part to adjusted pressing, they were able to turn the game around. What follows is an aspect analysis of Frankfurt’s attacking press against the Istanbul side’s build-up play.

Frankfurt started, as usual, in a nominal 4-2-3-1. Ahead of the two midfielders Chaibi and Larsson, Uzun operated in the No. 10 role. On the left flank, winger Knauff played in front of left-back Brown. When dropping into a deeper defensive press, this structure shifted into a 4-4-2, with Burkardt and either Uzun or Knauff forming the first pressing line. In the transition to the attacking press, they shifted man-oriented as usual.

Galatasaray also began in a nominal 4-2-3-1. In possession, their structure changed depending on the depth of their build-up, forming something like a 3-2-5 or 4-2-4 through the positioning of the full-backs. Width on the right side was provided by winger Sané, and on the left by full-back Elmalı. Left winger Akgün frequently moved inside, acting as a kind of “free player.” Meanwhile, right-back Sallai tucked in slightly in higher possession phases to help form a back three in the build-up.

Galatasaray’s build-up was characterized by frequent rotations. Their basic reference point was the previously described 3-2-5. From this shape, they consistently sought positional interchanges in order to manipulate the opponent’s man-marking in the pressing phase. On the left side, Elmalı, who provided width, was able to vary his height. In deeper build-up phases, he often dropped further back, while he also regularly positioned himself on the last line. Winger Akgün was tasked with creating central overloads and repeatedly moved inside, at times even drifting over to the right side. In response to the full-back pushing high, the left holding midfielder, Lemina, rotated into the left-back position. Striker Yılmaz, in turn, shifted into the left half-space when the full-back stayed deeper and the winger dropped centrally.

Galatasaray’s left-sided rotations force Frankfurt’s pressing players into constant reorientation.

The purpose of these rotations was to manipulate man-marking and tie down specific Frankfurt players. Because winger Akgün frequently dropped into central areas, the two centre-backs, Koch and Theate, were occupied. Akgün positioned himself in front of striker Yılmaz in order to stretch the last line and create 1v1 opportunities. This produced a variety of different build-up structures, which required Frankfurt to constantly reorient themselves. For example, if Elmalı pushed high, he had to be tracked by Collins, and Galatasaray’s four attacking players tied down Frankfurt’s entire back line. This significantly complicated Frankfurt’s transition into man-marking, as the positioning of the forwards forced centre-back Theate to cover long distances during handovers.

Since all players in the back line are occupied, defensive handovers become more difficult.

Frankfurt’s plan, as in previous matches, was to shift into man-marking from a +1 overload through the handover of the opponents’ centre-backs and full-backs. The advantage of this approach is that spaces on and behind the last line can initially be secured, while the risk of a long ball leading to a 1v1 situation is minimized by having an additional covering player.

At the same time, after steering the opponent’s attack, the transition into man-marking could be executed to apply maximum pressure on the ball carrier. Depending on the opponent’s structure and build-up sequences, Frankfurt adapted their pressing schemes. For instance, they could vary the distance to their opponents during the pressing jump: larger distances allowed for greater security in coverage, while smaller distances created more immediate pressure.

Galatasaray tried to make these handover moments as difficult as possible for Frankfurt by forcing the distances at the moment of transition into man-marking to become as large as possible. The aforementioned rotations, for example, forced Theate into long runs to pick up winger Sané, so that Brown could then jump out to the still unmarked and deeper-positioned right-back of Galatasaray.

Because of Galatasaray’s attackers’ positioning, Theate and Brown are forced into long pressing runs. On top of that, Theate finds himself caught between two opponents due to the rotations.

Galatasaray also tried to open up the center through certain rotations, aiming to draw Frankfurt’s midfielders out of central areas. Frankfurt tracked man-to-man with Larsson on half-space player Gündogan and Uzun on the deeper right-sided pivot Torreira. Depending on the side of build-up, Chaibi was prepared to jump onto the more advanced pivot, Lemina. Because Lemina often rotated into the full-back position, Doan frequently picked him up, leaving Chaibi or Collins (depending on the movements of Akgün and Elmalı) unexpectedly without a direct opponent. At the same time, Gündogan and Torreira sometimes rotated in opposite directions, which created difficulties for Frankfurt in terms of handovers in central areas.

Because Frankfurt found it increasingly difficult to press Galatasaray’s build-up through their usual handover approach, from around the 15th minute they opted for tighter marking distances to transition more quickly into full man-marking. Brown pushed up directly, Theate went straight onto Sané, while Collins and Koch marked the two remaining forwards. This created a 3v3 situation on the last line. Although this increased the pressure on Galatasaray and led to more turnovers in their build-up, it also raised the risk against long balls due to isolated 1v1s. Time and again, Galatasaray managed to exploit this by sending strong dribblers like Sané into such spacious 1v1 situations. Even so, the tighter marking distances resulted in a much more balanced game with more frequent ball recoveries.

In the 30th minute, Frankfurt used a longer stoppage to make more significant pressing adjustments. Winger Knauff no longer pressed Galatasaray’s right centre-back but instead oriented himself toward full-back Sallai. This allowed Brown to stay on Sané, and Theate no longer had to make long runs out wide to cover him. At the same time, it was no longer problematic if all four defenders were tied up, as they no longer needed to jump out of the back line. The free centre-back was now primarily pressed by Uzun, who used his cover shadow to block passing lanes into pivot Torreira. Galatasaray’s build-up on the right side could then be pressed man-to-man through ball-oriented shifting. To this end, Chaibi stepped onto Uzun’s original opponent, Torreira, and the far-side Doan also moved into the ball-near center.

Frankfurt’s pressing adjustments: With no need to shift across the last line anymore, Frankfurt gains situationally much better access with less risk. These changes spark the turnaround in the match.

The pressing adjustments led to significantly better situational access and, at the same time, to much stronger defensive security. Frankfurt were able to use the resulting ball recoveries to establish longer possession phases, which gave them a more dominant style of play. One such spell of possession resulted in the 2-1 lead shortly before halftime. Immediately afterwards, a ball recovery from the adjusted attacking press led to a free kick, which in turn produced the 3-1 at the break.

In the opening 30 minutes, Galatasaray were able to cause Frankfurt problems, thanks in part to a very elusive build-up play that challenged Frankfurt’s typical pressing approach with its reliance on handover moments. Frankfurt’s adjustments, however, proved perfectly suited to applying pressure more easily while also improving defensive cover. The short interruption of play came at just the right time for Toppmöller and his coaching staff to implement the necessary measures. As a result, the pressing changes were decisive for Frankfurt’s eventual 5-1 victory and a successful start to their Champions League campaign.

Author: MH is a football aficionado at heart. His apartment resembles a football library, with shelves filled with books on the great tacticians from Rinus Michels to Pep Guardiola. Of course, the book from Spielverlagerung.de is not missing. For MH, football is not just a game, it’s a way of life. He can be found on X under Mh_sv5 and LinkedIn.

#2748. Women Serie A | Lazio-Como, le formazioni ufficiali

Diramate le formazioni ufficiali di Lazio e Como in vista della quarta giornata di Serie A

In attesa dell’inizio della sfida Paolo Tramezzani e Gianluca Grassadonia hanno diramato le loro formazioni ufficiali in vista della sfida tra Lazio e Como, valida per la quarta giornata di Serie A Women Athora.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

LAZIO (3-5-2) : Mukasa; Consolini, Connolly, Martin, Zanoli, Castiello, Sciabica, Zannini, Monnecchi, Ngueleu, Visentin. A disposizione: Cetinja, Freeman, Holmstrøm, Masu, Camacho, Portales, Le Mouël, Longobardi, Goldoni, Mancini, Frenquellucci. Allenatore: Gianluca Grassadonia

#2746. Il Giornale - Il derby è la prima resa dei conti. O tormenti di Chivu per scoprire se...

Il Giornale - Il derby è la prima resa dei conti. O tormenti di Chivu per scoprire se...

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Prima il turno infrasettimanale e poi il derby. Inter e Milan si affronteranno sabato sera a San Siro ma dovranno prima superare rispettivamente il Venezia e il Bologna. Il Giornale parla delle contraddizioni delle due squadre. "Il derby passa da Venezia e dal Bologna, contro cui domani Inter e Milan rimettono in gioco le contraddizioni del momento. Le amnesie difensive dei nerazzurrinascoste sotto il tappeto dallo strapotere dell'attacco, le balbuzie europee dei rossoneri cancellate dall'imbattibilità in campionato".

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

"Otto giornate sono scorcio temporale insufficiente per valutare a fondo i valori, perché poche e perché differente è l'altezza degli ostacoli fin qui affrontati. (...) Il derby come prima resa dei conti,sapendo che gli spettatori interessati sono tanti. La classifica è corta e potrebbe esserlo ancora di più. Sarà il primo di Amorim, contando che l'Inter non lo vince da 7 partite (contro 3 allenatori diversi). I tormenti di Chivu per scoprire perché la sua squadra parte sempre in salita, non può bastargli dire che il calcio è questo ed è sempre stato così. Sette volte su 11 fanno statisticae sono troppe per essere un caso. Possono essere individuali, deficit di attenzione, errori tattici, certo è che prima l'allenatore nerazzurro risolve il rebus, prima la sua squadra può fare valere sul campo tutti i vantaggi che la critica gli attribuisce, considerando che già così, partendo 5 volte su 8 in svantaggio, è in testa al campionato", si legge sul quotidiano.

Chivu non avrà Lautaro, ancora infortunato. E il giornale parla così di Thuram che nella gara contro la Fiorentina ha trascinato l'Inter alla vittoria. "Chivu si consola con Thuram, uno che stupisce sempre ma che ogni anno è così, soprattutto parte sempre fortissimo. Deve decidere se farlo giocare anche in Laguna o se è più giusto evitargli la sesta consecutiva partita da titolare, perché poi il derby sarebbe la settima e perché prima della sosta ci saranno anche Feyenoord e Como. E dicono gli infortuni".

#2744. Real Madrid coach Jose Mourinho downplays defeating Barcelona: ‘We must not go over the moon’

Real Madrid secured a crucial victory against Barcelona on Matchday 10 of the 2026–27 La Liga season. However, manager Jose Mourinho firmly believes the squad must avoid complacency, reminding them that the job is far from finished and the domestic campaign remains long and grueling.

Propelled by a clinical brace from Kylian Mbappe—who reached the historic milestone of 100 goals in a Real Madrid shirt—Los Blancos marched into Spotify Camp Nou and walked away with three vital points in hand. Nevertheless, Madrid remain third in the La Liga standings, and Mourinho is acutely aware that his players must keep their feet firmly planted on the ground if they hope to lift silverware at the end of the season.

“In my view, Real Madrid do not play to win at the Camp Nou… Real Madrid play to win LaLiga,” Mourinho said after the game. “This must be the mentality. We need to continue like this and don’t go over the moon after this win.”

Entering Sunday’s showdown, Real Madrid trailed the Blaugrana by six points in the table. This away triumph allowed them to slash the deficit to just three points, keeping alive their ambitions of overtaking both Barcelona and second-place Atletico Madrid to claim the top spot before the mid-season winter break.

Achieving this victory without the suspended Vinicius Junior and starting goalkeeper Thibaut Courtois—who was sidelined on Sunday morning with a severe flu—showcased the squad’s tactical maturity and proved they do not rely solely on individual star names. With a healthier, deeper bench, Real Madrid are once again being viewed as genuine contenders both domestically and in the UEFA Champions League, where they maintain a strong European campaign.

Cristiano Ronaldo had one trait Kylian Mbappe approached differently, claims former teammate Ricardo Carvalho

Last season, recurring injuries severely undermined Real Madrid’s consistency and depth, limiting their ability to challenge on all fronts. If Mourinho can keep his core squad fit and firing over the grueling winter schedule, this Madrid team possesses every ingredient required to restore the dominant standard expected at the Santiago Bernabeu.

Jose Mourinho says Real Madrid will not change their structure for Lamine Yamal ahead of the Clasico against Barcelona.

With the forward facing criticism over his performances and a four-match La Liga suspension, Mourinho’s comments have raised fresh questions about the personal cost of remaining at one of soccer's biggest clubs.

The Spanish Football Federation's (RFEF) Disciplinary Committee has officially handed Vinicius Junior a four-match domestic suspension, and Real Madrid coach Jose Mourinho believes the timing of the announcement was off.

Jose Mourinho ruled out a January loan for Endrick, saying any club interested in the forward will have to fight him.

#2743. The Issue of Passivity – MX

von Next Generation am 25.10.2025 in den Kategorien Champions League mit 0 Kommentaren

A solid start was masked by an overly passive 5-4-1 and ultimately ended in a 5-1 defeat for Dino Toppmöller and Eintracht Frankfurt.

After three winless games, Eintracht Frankfurt faced Liverpool on Wednesday evening. Dino Toppmöller lined up in a 3-4-3 formation. Zetterer started in goal after extended discussions in the media. Amenda and Theate took up the half-back positions, while Koch operated as the central center-back. On the flanks, Kristensen and Brown were deployed. In midfield, Götze and Larsson formed the double-eight, with Doan and Bahoya on the wings and Knauff starting as the central striker.

The visitors from Liverpool arrived in Frankfurt with a similar situation: four consecutive defeats, including losses to underdogs like Crystal Palace and Istanbul. Arne Slot lined up in a 4-4-2 formation. Mamardashvili started in goal, with Konaté and van Dijk forming the central defense. On the flanks, Frimpong and Robertson defended, while Jones and Szoboszlai operated in central midfield as number eights. Gakpo and Wirtz occupied the wings, and up front, Isak and Ekitike formed the striking duo.

After the game, Markus Krösche said: “In the end, it’s about showing a completely different level of aggressiveness in the duels.” A topic that has been omnipresent around Frankfurt for weeks—and came into the spotlight again against Liverpool. But why did Frankfurt struggle so much to even engage in the duels?

This is a translated analysis. You can find the original here: https://spielverlagerung.de/2025/10/24/die-sache-mit-der-passivitaet-mx/

From the home side’s perspective, the game initially unfolded differently than the overall narrative suggested: it started off stable. In the first ten minutes, Dino Toppmöller’s side largely operated from a 3-4-3 structure in their higher build-up, with particular focus on the half-backs Theate and Amenda. Liverpool’s two forwards in the 4-4-2 mid-block struggled to gain access to the half-backs positioned in the half-spaces due to their narrow starting positions.

This repeatedly created horizontal pressing angles—i.e., side approaches—which carried a high risk of being dribbled past. Theate, in particular, was able to exploit this thanks to his very vertical first touch. Consequently, Liverpool’s number eights, especially Szoboszlai on Theate’s side, were often forced to move from the center into the half-space to press the advancing half-back. This opened central spaces next to them, into which Frankfurt’s number eights could advance and increasingly become available as passing options.

Hugo Larsson, in particular, was able to receive the ball from Theate multiple times, as the right number eight—Götze, or sometimes Doan and Knauff when positioned deeper—tied up Jones in Liverpool’s right half-space. This created, in the opening minutes, large pockets of space between Liverpool’s central number eights, allowing Larsson to push forward relatively freely.

This initially meant that Liverpool struggled to immediately disrupt Frankfurt’s build-up after the half-backs carried the ball forward. Often, the visitors responded by having Jones instinctively step out onto Larsson. However, due to the long pressing distance from the half-space into the center, he usually arrived late in the duel, allowing Larsson to hold onto the ball with his composure and often stabilize play via a relieving pass back to Theate. Jones stepping out also forced Gakpo, the far-side winger, to move into the half-space, reducing his ability to provide width for potential switches. Frankfurt, however, made too little direct use of these far-side spaces in the opening phase.

Despite dropping deeper, Larsson’s focus was also on another problematic area in Liverpool’s pressing: the space between the first and second lines. The narrow positioning of the two forwards is meant to isolate passing lanes from Frankfurt’s first build-up line into the defensive midfielder. In practice, though, their adjustment to players dropping behind them—beyond their tight starting positions—was limited.

Larsson frequently found pockets in the blind spot between the two forwards and became available through central center-back Koch in the defensive midfield zone. Szoboszlai and Jones were often tied up in the half-spaces by the inward-running Bahoya or Kristensen, and the gaps between Liverpool’s first and second pressing lines were relatively wide, preventing them from intuitively stepping out onto Larsson.

Against the forwards’ rearward pressing, the Swede was able to turn effectively and drive into the center with the ball. In some cases, this movement primarily served to draw Liverpool’s double-striker formation together, thereby giving the half-backs more space for diagonal dribbles—here, Larsson often simply laid the ball off. At other times, he actively sought progression and diagonal switches to advancing players in wide areas, most often targeting Brown on the left flank, which proved fairly effective in the opening phase.

However, Wirtz’s active rearward pressing limited space for Bahoya in the deep left half-space, meaning left-back Frimpong didn’t need to step out. As a result, Brown was frequently isolated on the wing, repeatedly facing 1v1 situations, which he struggled to handle consistently.

Liverpool Reveals Spaces Both Wide and Centrally

Speaking of Bahoya: He frequently looked to make diagonal runs into the space behind Szoboszlai to get into the center and be vertically available for Larsson. However, Larsson struggled noticeably in these situations to find Bahoya in the right spaces. On one hand, this was because during his diagonal dribbles he focused heavily on the flank, sometimes neglecting scanning toward the center. On the other hand, this orientation also exacerbated Brown’s isolation on the left wing: with Bahoya positioned centrally, there were often few nearby support options, while Theate rarely pushed up consistently in these moments. As a result, Brown was frequently isolated in 1v1 situations without passing options.

As Brown became increasingly involved in Frankfurt’s play, a new dynamic emerged: Wirtz pressed closer and more directly onto Brown, which opened up the vertical passing lane from Theate in the half-space to Bahoya, who repeatedly offered dropping movements.

At this point, one could discuss whether Liverpool’s pressing appeared too fragmented: on the one hand, the number eights were oriented too man-focused (see above), creating central spaces that were potentially exploitable. On the other hand, Wirtz’s wider positioning and Szoboszlai’s lack of compensatory movement meant these spaces were actively playable for Frankfurt. Or not?

On the other hand, it must be noted: what appears fragmented can also be well-prepared or intentional. While Bahoya was technically available as a passing option, Liverpool reacted very effectively in these situations. Right-back Frimpong tracked his dropping movements directly and consistently, while Wirtz and Szoboszlai stepped up, creating situations in the half-space where Bahoya faced 1v2 or even 1v3 disadvantages, simultaneously closing potential lateral passing options. Considering that Bahoya is primarily a player who looks to provide vertical depth rather than withstand direct pressure, this approach made sense: Brown was pressed more directly, Theate was forced into the half-space, and Bahoya came under immediate pressure, often leaving him with only the option of a backward pass.

Wirtz’s more direct stepping up also addressed an issue from the opening minutes. Frankfurt’s narrow starting positions had allowed Brown to remain available laterally to Theate, creating a very horizontal pressing angle. This enabled Brown to open up vertically and play deeper to Bahoya or carry the ball himself—something that ideally should have been prevented.

After Frankfurt’s relatively stable start—facilitated by higher initial possession that gradually decreased due to enforced verticality—Liverpool increasingly found their rhythm. This was mainly because Frankfurt allowed it. The Eagles set up in a 5-4-1 deep block, prioritizing blocking rather than actively pressing. This was particularly evident with striker Knauff: he lightly tracked Liverpool’s half-space dribbles but rarely contested the duels actively. Consequently, there was no isolation on lateral passes from the first pressing line, allowing Liverpool to shift play between the center-backs and repeatedly dribble forward. Frankfurt’s midfield was forced into constant lateral shifts, which repeatedly opened spaces within the 5-4-1 block.

Liverpool thus created excellent conditions to exploit gaps inside Frankfurt’s 5-4-1 structure. Both number eights, Jones and Szoboszlai, occasionally broke forward ahead of Frankfurt’s second pressing line, acting as part of the first build-up line between center-backs and half-backs (or “wing backs”), creating temporary 5-0-5/3-2-5 spacing. The intent was twofold: first, technically strong players like Szoboszlai and Jones could dribble and exploit small diagonal spaces; second, the expansive build-up created direct backward passing options via central center-back van Dijk, enabling quicker lateral switches and further straining Frankfurt’s shifting.

The main aim, however, was to position as many players as possible around the block—both in front and behind—to force Frankfurt’s number eights to step out reactively onto Liverpool’s dribblers in the half-space, while simultaneously opening “blind-spot” spaces in front of the defensive line. Structurally, this was facilitated on the right by Konaté, who could push Szoboszlai wider from the half-right position. This, in turn, pulled Frankfurt’s left winger Bahoya slightly out of compactness (likely to prevent a 2v1 in width behind him), while Larsson was forced into more active blocking—slightly stepping up without actively contesting duels, creating minimal defensive shadow. As a result of these positional adjustments, spaces often emerged between Bahoya and Larsson, which Florian Wirtz sought diagonally.

This created some interesting patterns in the lay-off play. Ekitiké occasionally moved into the number 10 zone, positioning himself horizontally to the right following Wirtz’s diagonal runs, allowing Wirtz to lay the ball off to him. Since Wirtz immediately stepped back into the “play & move” rhythm, these pressure situations occasionally opened opportunities for direct forward progression into deeper areas—even if they were not fully exploited in the opening phase.

It also became apparent that a defensive focus on blocking inherently involves some degree of reactivity. Drops from the last line were not actively tracked but responded to reactively. The priority was securing depth first and defending only upon receiving passes. This approach gave Wirtz and Ekitiké additional freedom in the half-space—players of their quality can exploit such pockets effectively. This was particularly noticeable in the first-touch lay-off patterns.

Because Liverpool could switch the ball with ease, it was increasingly played from one wing-back to another. Personnel shifts were very dynamic: Jones, Wirtz, or Szoboszlai occasionally operated wide as wing-backs, while Konaté and Robertson advanced into the half-spaces. Switches could be executed directly through the half-space movements of van Dijk. The core issue for Frankfurt lay in the 5-4-1 block: ball-far wingers and full-backs tended to drift too far into the half-space. After Liverpool’s switches, these players moved relatively uncontrollably back into width to track their direct opponents.

At this point, the question arises as to why Frankfurt focused so heavily on blocking and, after deliberately allowing switches, responded in such a fragmented way by pushing out wide.

This repeatedly created pockets of space between full-back and half-back that Frankfurt’s 5-4-1 block was supposed to prevent, but Ekitiké consistently sought and exploited them between Theate and Brown. The issue was compounded by the fact that, although Frankfurt tended to push out, the long pressing distance in width made it difficult to prevent Liverpool from seeking 1v1 situations. Brown was repeatedly placed under intense pressure in these duels, as diagonal pressing in a deep block is one of the most complex tasks: he needed to both close depth to block dribbles and runs from the half-space and defend the flank. Early on, however, Brown managed this reasonably well, and a subsequent ball win from him led to Frankfurt’s opening goal.

Frankfurt’s wide players increasingly dropped back during Liverpool possession to support the full-backs in loose numerical superiority pressing. Yet, Doan on the right frequently failed to isolate the backward passing lane, often losing track of Jones and Robertson, allowing Gakpo to break free and enable another switch. On the left, the rotational setup was less dynamic, but Gakpo was slightly more proactive than Frimpong (later Bradley) in taking on Kristensen directly in 1v1s. Initially, however, there was insufficient depth support, leaving Gakpo relatively isolated. Kristensen’s effective blocking of crossing options further limited the Dutchman’s effectiveness in these situations.

After conceding, Liverpool pushed further forward, particularly in how their wing/full-back (depending on Szoboszlai’s temporary role in the back three) and number eights operated higher. Robertson and Jones increasingly formed a pair on the left side, while Gakpo moved more centrally in response to his earlier isolated position. Robertson pushed up as the left wing-back to free himself from Doan’s pressure and, together with Jones, create a 2v1 against Kristensen. This allowed Gakpo to repeatedly hint at depth in the half-space or actively position himself in the box—crucial since crosses had previously been only partially effective.

The width pair also allowed Doan to drop more frequently and shift from the half-space into wider positions, reopening spaces in the ball-side half-space. These were exploited primarily by Wirtz when diagonally dropping, forming a kind of dynamic trio with rotating coverage of width and half-space alongside Gakpo and Robertson. Typically, Wirtz, as the trigger for Phase 1 dynamics, dropped diagonally into the half-space and laid off to Robertson moving forward, while Jones pushed diagonally into the half-space center behind Wirtz, later being targeted by Robertson as the receiving player.

the center. This splitting of the back five deliberately opened up deep half-space channels behind Frankfurt’s defensive line. At the same time, the Reds often sought to occupy the ball-far full-back and half-back, creating central spaces. Isak exploited these spaces with diagonal runs, using his pace advantage over Theate effectively.

When these spaces couldn’t be played directly, Liverpool remained in motion. One of Liverpool’s key strengths is maintaining this dynamic play through constant rotation of width. For example, Wirtz moved wide when the deep ball didn’t arrive, seeking 1v1 situations against Kristensen. The Frankfurt defender struggled against the German, who consistently cut inside. Through these inverted dribbles, Wirtz repeatedly found half-space areas—often connecting with Jones or Gakpo, who were probing the space in front of the defensive line. Frankfurt’s defenders continued to struggle with decisions between stepping out and securing depth.

It also became clear that the wing-backs increasingly made overlapping runs when the wingers had the ball. For full-back Kristensen, this meant he no longer consistently engaged directly with the winger, often leaving the duel to Doan to track the ball, while he dealt with the overlapping runner. Consequently, Frankfurt’s lateral dueling became more passive, giving the winger more space and time on the ball. Simultaneously, channels opened centrally in front of the defensive line because Kristensen was occupied by the overlapping full-back and Doan’s wide diagonal drop failed to fully isolate the passing lane to the center. This allowed Liverpool increasingly comfortable ball circulation on the wings with central connection options, opening opportunities for shots from the edge of the box—especially for the number eights, who could join centrally and occasionally shoot from distance.

This increasingly prominent Liverpool dynamic, combined with Frankfurt’s continued failure to isolate backward passes, forced Frankfurt in the middle of the first half to operate deeper in their 5-4-1 block, constantly defending in a low posture. The original plan—to lure Liverpool out, provoke errors into the compact block, and then attack their rest defense on the counter—became progressively more difficult to execute.

This was largely because Frankfurt’s wide players, Bahoya and Doan, were increasingly drawn into defensive duties and had to drop deep, meaning they couldn’t provide the vertical width after winning the ball that they typically should as deep runners. At the same time, Liverpool tracked any vertical movements from Frankfurt’s wide players very effectively through their wing-backs; on the ball-far side (here Bradley), they aimed to anticipate the advance of the far-side winger and mark him with the far-side wing-back, making it difficult for him to break free.

Additionally, Liverpool used their deeper number eight—usually Szoboszlai—and van Dijk to double up on the ball-near attacker Knauff in the half-space, preventing him from being played into effectively, which worked well: Knauff struggled to hold the ball for wall passes after turnovers. Konaté covered any spaces behind this double, particularly for second balls. However, the underlying problem remained that blocking implicitly assumes winning the ball within the defensive line when defending deep. After ball recoveries, Frankfurt could hardly progress from the back against Liverpool’s immediate numerical overload, which instantly triggered overloading counter-pressing and closed off vertical passing options to the number eights—the weak point in Liverpool’s rest defense system. As a result, Frankfurt’s defenders were often forced into long balls under pressure.

In other words, even after going 1-0 down, Frankfurt continued to lose possession. Because they operated deeper, these turnovers occurred in more advanced positions of Liverpool’s pressing, creating direct pressure and limiting structured buildup. The space that needed to be covered on counters was larger than in the mid-block early in the game.

One could also discuss how a counter-attacking approach correlates with pressing and collective compactness. Frankfurt occasionally pressed individually and fragmentedly in response to certain cues (e.g., second dribbles in the half-space or wide distances), but lacked a clear, collective transition from the block into pressing. This allowed Liverpool to face minimal pressure in their first build-up line and develop their dynamic play, effectively freezing Frankfurt into passive blocking.

Andrzejewski et al. (2022), in Analysis of team success based on match technical and running performance in a professional soccer league, analyzed over 600 Bundesliga games across two seasons and correlated various performance indicators with team success. One key point: more duels won correlated with more counterattacks. Similarly, Lepschy et al. (2021) found in analyses of the 2014 and 2018 World Cups that teams with higher duel success and effective counters were more successful. These findings suggest that both the quality and quantity of duels are linearly linked to successful counterattacking.

At the same time, Liverpool created relatively few chances from open play. They generated five major chances, with two goals from set-pieces and one counter leading to a 3-1 halftime lead. Their main struggles were in finishing crosses and chance creation around the box. A side effect of their play around Frankfurt’s block was that much creative personnel was positioned wide, limiting their effectiveness in tight spaces. Players like Wirtz and Jones rotated around the block with the wide players, sometimes causing problems in small-sided scenarios inside Frankfurt’s compactness. These options were therefore missing in key areas.

Build-up should not be judged solely by whether it leads to goals but also by how it shifts game momentum. This increasingly shifted in Liverpool’s favor, largely due to their adjusted progressive play.

After two corner goals just before halftime—each exploiting target players (van Dijk and Konaté) to find the “blind spot” created by horizontal movements of blockers like Ekitiké and Robertson—little else occurred in the first half. Liverpool continued to control the game against Frankfurt’s deep block, increasingly using direct diagonal switches to the far side to give their wide players more time 1v1 against Frankfurt’s ball-far full-backs or to create depth in the box.

These passes were occasionally imprecise, causing “ping-pong” situations in front of Frankfurt’s box around the central number eights. Liverpool faced minor counter-pressing problems but could usually manage them with collective back-pressing and tactical fouls.

At halftime, Arne Slot brought on Chiesa for Isak, and Liverpool increasingly adopted a clear back-three setup in a 3-5-2 with slight adjustments. Wing-backs Robertson and Bradley operated deeper in their starting positions, intending to pull Frankfurt’s full-backs wider and open half-space channels for the inside wingers Wirtz and Gakpo. Diagonal breaking movements—acting as connectors between wing-back and number eight—remained visible, particularly Wirtz on the right. During ball possession, he would pull Konaté out, quickly lay off to Bradley, and then exploit the space between Brown and Theate while moving in the “play & go” rhythm.

Overall, Eintracht also struggled in the second half to consistently track or cover the drops from their last line in front of the second block. This repeatedly created 2v1 overload situations for Liverpool in width. Theate occasionally pushed out slightly but could not exert meaningful pressure on Wirtz. Instead, he often opened spaces in behind, which Chiesa repeatedly exploited to be played directly in behind—often from passes by Konaté.

Direct diagonal balls to advancing wing-backs became more common, with these players benefiting from slightly deeper starting positions to gain a dynamic advantage over Frankfurt’s wide defenders (Brown in particular struggled against Bradley), allowing these balls to reach deep positions. The persistent issue, however, remained that Frankfurt struggled to get into the box.

Frankfurt offered very little themselves. Apart from isolated actions following ball recoveries that couldn’t be converted directly into counterattacks, little developed. Even during brief periods of higher possession, the team remained vulnerable to Liverpool counters. This was primarily because Frankfurt’s number eights continued to operate high, leaving the space in front of the first-second line rest defense largely uncovered. Fast attackers—especially Ekitiké—and the dribble-capable Chiesa consistently exploited these half-space channels in Liverpool’s secondary attacks.

The 4-1 goal came from a Liverpool turnover after Szoboszlai freed himself in the number eight zone with a dribble. Wirtz received the ball deep in the half-space between Brown and Theate. Gakpo drove forward from the space in front of Frankfurt’s number eights to the far post, exploiting the goalkeeper’s “blind spot,” and finished. (The goalkeeper’s blind spot is often an underestimated reference point for attackers.) The 5-1 was a long-range shot from Szoboszlai following a horizontal lay-off from the wide area into the half-space. Such lay-offs occurred frequently throughout the game but had rarely been finished consistently.

Overall, it was an interesting game—predictable in some ways but also grinding at times. Frankfurt must significantly improve their defensive activity and pressing to get back on track in the coming weeks, particularly against top teams. Liverpool, on the other hand, secured important morale-boosting points. The team showed clear potential for improvement, but also remained prone to errors. Overall, it was a favorable game for Slot and Liverpool.

MX made a name for himself in Regensburg with his tendency to overcomplicate the game. While flirting with the RB school, he remained a secret romantic at heart, drawn to Guardiola’s style of football.

#2742. FIFA: United States to host first-ever Women’s Cup World Cup in 2028

Additionally, FIFA stated that each of the 32 teams competing at the Women’s World Cup next summer will have $2.5 million in preparation funds.

FIFA has officially unveiled the United States as the host of the first-ever FIFA Women’s Club World Cup in 2028. No cities have been determined, with the announcement of individual city selections to come at a later date.

However, seven teams have been confirmed for the Club World Cup: Arsenal WFC (England), Club América Femenil (Mexico), FC Barcelona Femení (Spain), Gotham FC (United States), Naegohyang WFC (DPR Korea), SC Corinthians (Brazil) and Wuhan Jiangda WFC (China PR).

The Women’s Club World Cup will take place from January 5-30, 2028.

Additionally, during the FIFA Bureau of the Council meeting, the organization announced that each of the 2027 FIFA Women’s World Cup’s 32 teams will receive $2.5 million in preparation money — which is the same amount as the 48 teams that competed in the 2026 FIFA Men’s World Cup.

Regarding the Club World Cup, FIFA stated that, “throughout this year, the FIFA administration has worked to identify a strong host for the maiden edition of the quadrennial competition, which will feature 19 of the best women’s club teams hailing from all six confederations.”

FIFA Chief Women’s Fotoball Officer Dame Sarai Bareman stated that the United States’ recent success in hosting the 2026 World Cup and other “top FIFA tournaments” resulted in the nation’s selection, along with “the significant interest in women’s football there plus its elite sporting infrastructure.”

“Alongside the vibrant and passionate women’s football ecosystem in the United States, the qualified clubs, government, host cities, our commercial and media partners and more, our mission will be to set an impressive standard for global women’s club football and a benchmark for the future,” Bareman said.

The following 12 clubs will be announced at a later date. According to FIFA, the qualification model “rewards sporting performance by prioritizing continental champions spanning seasons ending in the years 2024 to 2027.”

Bareman said that she is excited to see the seven clubs and remaining clubs “show the strength and depth of women’s club football” to a global audience.

“They will have the opportunity to tell their story, expand their fan base and represent their club on the world stage,” she said. “They have thoroughly earned this opportunity to be part of history in the United States.”

The remaining spots will be allocated as:

#2741. Marmoush Scores First Tottenham Goal Against Coventry

Omar Marmoush scored his first goal for Tottenham Hotspur as they beat Coventry City, ending their six-match winless start to the season.

The Egyptian forward struck in the 59th minute at Tottenham Hotspur Stadium, restoring Spurs’ lead shortly after Coventry had equalised.

The victory gave Roberto De Zerbi’s side their first league win of the campaign. Tottenham moved onto six points after seven matches, having previously recorded three draws and three defeats.

Marmoush’s goal also ended his personal wait for a first competitive strike since joining Spurs from Manchester City in August.

James Maddison gave Tottenham the lead in the 14th minute with a long-range strike, marking his first competitive start in 536 days.

Mohammed Kudus provided the assist as Maddison celebrated his return from a lengthy injury absence.

Coventry responded five minutes after the restart when Jack Rudoni finished a move involving Brandon Thomas-Asante to make it 1-1.

However, Marmoush restored Tottenham’s advantage nine minutes later. Andy Robertson supplied the Egyptian, who curled a shot beyond goalkeeper Carl Rushworth from outside the penalty area.

As the match came to a close, Ben Davies scored a third goal in the 96th minute. Coventry had pushed for another equaliser in the closing stages, but Spurs held on to claim all three points.

Marmoush joined Tottenham on August 27 on a season-long loan from Manchester City, with an obligation to make the transfer permanent next summer.

He had since struggled to make an impact in front of goal as Tottenham endured a difficult opening to the season. Before the international break, Marmoush acknowledged that Spurs’ attacking players were still adapting to De Zerbi’s demands.

Spurs next travel to Stamford Bridge to face Chelsea on October 24.

#2737. Le vittorie dell'Inter da sfavorita che hanno fatto la storia

Le vittorie dell'Inter da sfavorita che hanno fatto la storia

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Vincere da favoriti è quasi un dovere. Vincere quando tutti danno per certo il successo dell'avversario è un'altra faccenda, ed è lì che l'Inter ha scritto alcune delle sue pagine più belle. Tra le vittorie dell'Inter ce ne sono alcune che pesano più di altre proprio perché nessuno le aveva previste: finali contro squadre che dominavano l'Europa da anni, semifinali giocate in dieci uomini, coppe alzate in stagioni in cui il campionato faceva paura. Qui ripercorriamo quattro imprese nerazzurre arrivate contro i pronostici, dal Prater di Vienna del 1964 alla notte di San Siro contro il Barcellona nel 2025. Per ognuna ci sono i protagonisti, i numeri e il motivo per cui l'Inter partiva indietro. Prima, però, conviene chiarire cosa voglia dire davvero essere sfavoriti.

Oggi lo sfavore si legge anche nelle quote, che traducono in numeri la probabilità assegnata a ogni esito. Un valore unico, però, non esiste. Anche molti casinò online con licenza AAMS hanno ormai una sezione dedicata allo sport, e con così tanti operatori sul mercato la stessa partita può essere quotata in modo diverso da un sito all'altro. Non a caso esistono portali che analizzano i casinò online mettendo a confronto operatori e condizioni. Ai tempi di Herrera, invece, bastava sfogliare i giornali della vigilia per capire chi partiva avanti. E nelle partite che seguono, quasi mai era l'Inter.

La squadra di Herrera colpì poco prima dell'intervallo con un ribaltamento di fronte rapidissimo: Facchetti servì Sandro Mazzola, che firmò l'1-0 al 43'. Aurelio Milani raddoppiò al 60', Felo accorciò per gli spagnoli e Mazzola chiuse i conti al 76'. Davanti a oltre 71mila spettatori finiva 3-1 e nasceva ufficialmente la Grande Inter, che un anno dopo avrebbe bissato il titolo battendo il Benfica a San Siro. La seconda coppa fu una conferma. Quella di Vienna resta invece il momento in cui un'outsider si prese l'Europa.

Eppure in Coppa UEFA la stessa squadra mostrava una solidità che in Serie A non si vedeva mai. La finale contro il Salisburgo si giocò su due gare: all'andata, il 26 aprile a Vienna, decise un gol di Nicola Berti, al ritorno, l'11 maggio a San Siro, bastò una rete di Jonk. Doppio 1-0 e seconda Coppa UEFA della storia nerazzurra. Fu anche l'ultima partita di Walter Zenga con l'Inter, che anni dopo avrebbe ricordato quella sera come l'addio perfetto davanti a uno stadio pieno. Una squadra che aveva rischiato la Serie B chiudeva la stagione con un trofeo europeo in bacheca: difficile immaginare un esempio più netto di pronostico ribaltato.

Il 28 aprile, davanti a quasi centomila spettatori, la serata si complicò subito. Pandev si fermò all'ultimo momento e al suo posto giocò Cristian Chivu, oggi allenatore dei nerazzurri. Al 28' Thiago Motta venne espulso dopo un contatto con Busquets e l'Inter rimase in dieci per oltre un'ora. Da quel momento fu una partita di pura resistenza, con la squadra schiacciata nella propria metà campo. Il gol di Piqué all'84' riaccese il Camp Nou, ma non bastò: con il 3-2 complessivo l'Inter volò in finale. Il Triplete si sarebbe completato il 22 maggio a Madrid grazie alla doppietta di Milito contro il Bayern Monaco. La sconfitta più dolce della storia nerazzurra, però, resta quella di Barcellona.

La squadra di Simone Inzaghi chiuse il primo tempo sul 2-0 con Lautaro Martínez e un rigore di Calhanoglu. Nella ripresa il Barcellona rimontò fino al 3-2, con Eric García, Dani Olmo e Raphinha all'87'. Al 93' Francesco Acerbi, salito in attacco per l'assalto finale, trovò il pareggio, e ai supplementari Davide Frattesi firmò il 4-3 al 99'. Le statistiche dicono quanto fosse squilibrata la gara: l'Inter chiuse con il 29% di possesso palla contro il 71% degli spagnoli. La finale di Monaco contro il PSG andò in tutt'altro modo, ma quella semifinale è entrata di diritto tra le notti più intense mai vissute al Meazza.

Il soprannome, però, non se n'è mai andato davvero. Nella stagione 2025/26, dopo aver rimontato due volte uno 0-2 contro il Como nel giro di dieci giorni, Cristian Chivu l'ha rivendicato apertamente, indicando in quello spirito la chiave delle due rimonte. Quella stagione si è chiusa con scudetto e Coppa Italia, la prova che una squadra può essere solida e conservare lo stesso istinto delle notti di Vienna e del Camp Nou. In fondo è questo il filo che lega sessant'anni di imprese: quando il pronostico la dà per spacciata, l'Inter trova spesso la sua versione migliore.

#2735. Why was Cristiano Ronaldo omitted from the 2026 Ballon d’Or 30-man shortlist?

Cristiano Ronaldo has enjoyed a storied career spanning more than two decades. However, the Portuguese icon has now been omitted from the 2026 Ballon d’Or shortlist in a decision that has taken many soccer supporters by surprise.

Ronaldo failed to make the 30-man cut after the voting panel determined his campaign was not sufficiently dominant to rank among the world’s elite over the qualifying period. While he enjoyed a prolific individual season with Al Nassr, the Saudi Pro League continues to face skepticism from global voters regarding its relative competitiveness.

By contrast, Julian Quinones of Mexico did earn a nomination while playing in the Saudi Pro League with Al Qadsiah, having finished the domestic campaign as the league’s top scorer. Quinones also anchored Mexico’s attack during the 2026 FIFA World Cup, finishing as his nation’s top marksman and leading El Tri on a historic knockout-stage run on home soil.

Ronaldo was last nominated for the Ballon d’Or in 2022. He finished 20th in the global voting that year, an edition that saw former Real Madrid teammate Karim Benzema capture the first and only Ballon d’Or of his career.

Ronaldo joined Al Nassr in January 2023. While his scoring output for the Saudi side has remained remarkably consistent, his inability to deliver major silverware at club level alongside more subdued tournament showings with the Portugal national team have seen him omitted from the shortlist in four consecutive editions: 2023, 2024, 2025, and now 2026.

Cristiano Ronaldo celebrates Al Nassr’s 71st anniversary with message to the club

Since falling out of contention for the shortlist, Ronaldo has publicly dismissed the significance of individual accolades. The veteran striker has noted on several occasions that subjective voting bodies—composed of journalists and media figures whom he feels harbor personal biases—undermine the true sporting credibility of the award.

Cristiano Ronaldo has claimed five Ballon d’Or awards across his illustrious career. He holds the all-time record for career nominations with 18, with his most recent triumph arriving in 2017.

Ronaldo lifted the coveted trophy in 2008, 2013, 2014, 2016, and 2017. Now in the twilight of his playing days, the forward has shifted his ambitions away from gala podiums and onto the pitch, channeling his full focus toward surpassing the unprecedented milestone of 1,000 career goals before hanging up his boots.

Kane’s historic 2025-26 campaign helped him secure the Ballon d’Or, while Messi’s extraordinary achievements continue to provide the benchmark against which the game’s greatest goalscorers are measured.

Harry Kane has secured his first Ballon d'Or at 33 after capturing the 2026 edition, but how does his age compare to that of Lionel Messi and Cristiano Ronaldo when they first won the award?

Lamine Yamal made history by claiming the Kopa Trophy for the third consecutive year, but can the 19-year-old phenom still cap his night by lifting the 2026 Ballon d'Or?

The 2026 Ballon d'Or winner will be unveiled today, Monday, October 26, as world soccer's most prestigious individual award takes center stage at the historic London Palladium. Take a look at all the details to live stream it in the United States.
